Section 5.  That sub-section 6.2.2.a. is amended to read as follows: “The standards in this section 
shall apply to all new construction and changes of use in all zoning districts, except that no parking is 
required in the CBD district or the UU Overlay District. When sites do elect to provide parking in the CBD 
district or the UU Overlay District, all maximum quantity, location, design standards and specific 
limitations in this Article shall apply.” 
 
REVIEW CRITERIA: 
 
Review of a UDC text amendment shall be measured by the following three (3) criteria of Section 2.4.1.d: 
 
1. COMPREHENSIVE PLAN:  
“The amendment is in accordance with general goals and policies of the Comprehensive Plan, or is 
necessary to address an emerging issue not anticipated in the Comprehensive Plan and is not 
inconsistent with the plan.” 
 
This proposed text amendment is consistent with the principles and policies within PlanCheyenne. 
Specifically, the proposed amendment addresses the following policies and principles: 
 
• 
Policy 1.1.B: Flexibility to Respond to Market Demands. 
The UU Overlay text amendment will provide flexibility with zoning regulations to promote 
infill development, adaptive reuse, and redevelopment of an important area in the City of 
Cheyenne. The flexibility should incentivize the market to consider developing in the West 
Edge. 
 
• 
Policy 1.4.A: Infill Opportunities 
The Urban Use Overlay text amendment provides the opportunity for infill development 
through regulations that promote flexibility, but also consider the character of the area in 
which they are located. 
 
• 
Policy 1.4.C: Downtown Revitalization and Mix of Uses 
The Urban Use Overlay can be applicable to the MUR, MUB, and P zone districts in or adjacent 
to downtown. As the Urban Use Overlay is applied to the West Edge it is anticipated to 
revitalize the area and further mix uses in the area. Staff anticipates the development of the 
West Edge will greatly benefit the downtown as a whole. 
 
 
• 
Policy 1.4.D: Infill and Revitalization Incentives 
The Urban Use overlay achieves several items that are mentioned in this policy; such as 
reducing parking minimums, providing flexible dimensional standards, promoting higher 
densities than currently permitted, and providing flexibility in design requirements for 
utilizing existing buildings. 
 
• 
Policy 2.3.A: Mix of Housing Types 
The Urban Use Overlay will lead to the West Edge being zoned MUB or MUR; each of which 
provide ample opportunities for a variety of housing. 

PLN-20-00011  
 
 
May 17, 2021 
 
 
• 
Policy 3.2.B: Active Uses in the Street Level 
The Urban Use Overlay promotes active uses on the street level by permitting various street 
furnishings and projections into the Right of Way. 
 
• 
Principle 4.3: The Cheyenne Area will build a safe, multimodal transportation system that 
consists of streets, sidewalks, bicycle facilities, and transit 
The Urban Use Overlay promotes bicycle infrastructure and pedestrian amenities through 
increased bicycle parking and an active pedestrian zone. 
 
• 
Policy 5.1.A: Historic Building Restoration 
The Urban Use Overlay promotes building reuse and restoration with flexible site design and 
building placement requirements for utilizing existing buildings.  
 
2. STATE STATUTORY REQUIREMENTS:  
The amendment is consistent with the Wyoming Statutes, and specifically the Municipal Planning 
and Zoning statutes. The following Articles are all listed verbatim from Wyoming State Statute. 
 
WYO 15-1-602 (a):  
The governing body shall specify how regulations, restrictions and the 
district boundaries are to be determined, established, enforced, 
amended, supplemented or otherwise changed. 
Assessment:  
State statute states that the governing body shall specify how regulations 
regarding zoning are established and enforced.  The governing body 
possesses the authority to amend regulations and therefore can amend 
the Unified Development Code to reflect this text amendment. 
 
WYO 15-1-601 (a):  
The governing body of any city or town, by ordinance, may: 
(i) Regulate and restrict the: 
(D) Density of the population 
(E) Location and use of buildings, structures and land for trade, 
industry, residence or other purposes. 
Assessment:  
This amendment proposes modifying the Unified Development Code for 
the purposes of regulating the location of buildings and density of the 
population; thus, fulfilling the requirements of this statute.  
 
WYO 15-1-601(d):  
All regulations shall be made: 
(ii) With reasonable consideration, among other things, of the character 
of the district and its peculiar suitability for particular uses; 
(iii) With a view to conserving the value of buildings and encouraging the 
most appropriate use of land throughout the city or town. 
Assessment: 
This amendment proposes modifying the Unified Development Code for 
the purposes of encouraging redevelopment and infill development in 
the City of Cheyenne with respect to the existing character of the districts 
to which these regulations are applied. Furthermore, these regulations 
contain provisions for height regulations to preserve views of existing 
prominent structures. Regulations of this nature are allowable under 
state statue and may be modified by the Governing Body.    
 
 

PLN-20-00011  
 
 
May 17, 2021 
 
3. HEALTH, SAFETY, AND PUBLIC WELFARE:  
“The amendment promotes the general health, safety and public welfare.” 
 
This amendment will promote general health, safety, and public welfare by reinvesting in the 
community and promoting walkable, mixed-use development. Furthermore, it is anticipated that 
this amendment will promote much needed housing development in the community.  
 
 
CONCLUSION:  
 
The Urban Use Overlay district provides an effective framework for redevelopment in near-downtown 
neighborhoods in Cheyenne, specifically in the West Edge District. The proposed amendments have been 
vetted through significant public outreach and substantially conform to Plan Cheyenne and the West Edge 
Area plans. Adoption of the Urban Use Overlay is an important step in West Edge redevelopment and staff 
concludes that this text amendment meets the review criteria of UDC 2.4.1.d.  
 
 
STAFF RECOMMENDATION:  
 
Staff recommends approval of text amendments to the Unified Development Code to create the Urban 
Use Overlay District, noting the request meets the review criteria of UDC 2.4.1.d.

ORDINANCE NO. ___________ 
 
ENTITLED: “AN ORDINANCE CREATING SECTION 13.04.141, FEE ASSESSMENT 
ON MONTHLY GROSS REVENUES, OF CHAPTER 13.04, BOARD OF 
PUBLIC UTILITIES, OF TITLE 13, PUBLIC UTILITIES GENERALLY, 
OF THE CODE OF THE CITY OF CHEYENNE, WYOMING.” 
 
BE IT ORDAINED BY THE GOVERNING BODY OF THE CITY OF CHEYENNE, 
WYOMING:  
 
Section 1.  That Section 13.04.141, Fee assessment on monthly gross revenues, of Chapter 
13.04, Board of Public Utilities, of Title 13, Public Utilities Generally, of the Code of the City of 
Cheyenne, Wyoming, is hereby created as set out in this ordinance.   
13.04.141 
Fee assessment on monthly gross revenues.     
 
 Effective July 1, 2021 and continuing thereafter, the board shall collect on behalf of the 
city a two percent (2%) fee on monthly gross revenues of water and sewage services and remit 
those fees to the city treasurer as a pass through. 
Section 2. 
That this ordinance shall be in full force and effect on July 1, 2021.

STAFF REPORT 
 
FILE: 
UDC-21-00197:  Annexation of Meadowlark Estates, 2nd Filing  
REQUEST: 
An annexation of 17.8 acres of land (21.8 if Dell Range right-of-way is included)
LOCATION: 
Northeast of and adjacent to the intersection of Dell Range Boulevard and 
Whitney Road.  
APPLICANT: 
PKT Development LLC  Butch Tilton 
AGENT: 
Bruce Perryman, AVI, PC  
OWNER: 
Meadowlark Estates LLC, c/o PKT Development LLC 
PURPOSE: 
To allow future urban-level development 
PREPARED BY: 
Seth Lloyd, Planner II  Development 
 
 
 
 
  
  
  
  
  Planning and Development Department 
 
(Phone) 307-637-6282 
(Fax) 307-637-6366 

UDC-21-00197
June 14, 2021
APPLICABLE CITY CODE SECTION(S) AND PLANS: 
 
 
UDC 2.4.2 Annexations 
 
PlanCheyenne 
 
East Dell Range Blvd / US 30 Corridor Study 
 
Whitney Road Corridor Plan 
 
BACKGROUND: 
 
The applicant is requesting annexing of 17.8 acres of land for future residential and commercial 
development. The County Assessor presently shows the tract of land as part of one larger unplatted parcel. 
The land is currently undeveloped in the County. A zone change is proposed concurrently with this 
annexation. Both applications are anticipated to come to final consideration before the Governing Body at 
the same meeting.  
 
Land annexed to the City requires a City zone. The petition to annex has been accompanied by a submission 
to change the zoning from County MU Mixed-Use and County LR Low-density Residential to the City zones 
of CB Community Business, LR Low-density Residential, and MR Medium-density Residential. The purpose of 
this annexation is to allow future urban development un
. A preliminary plat for the 
area, with lots and blocks designed for residential and commercial development, has also been submitted. It 
is anticipated that a final plat would come before the Governing Body within a year of this annexation. 
 
SURROUNDING LAND USE AND ZONING: 
 
 
The property is currently zoned MU Mixed-Use and LR Low-density Residential in the County. The 
property is concurrently proposed to be annexed and rezoned to the CB Community Business, LR Low-
density Residential, and MR Medium-density Residential zones. The CB zone is intended for retail, 
employment, service and civic uses that may serve a number of residential neighborhoods. Typically, 
areas zoned CB are expected to be more automobile-oriented and serve the region. Commercial 
development at this intersection is anticipated by the properties mixed-use future land use designation, 
and any commercial development at this intersection would likely have a regional service area (including 
the surrounding rural and low-to-medium density residential developments). Both the LR and MR zones 
are intended for residential development with access to public infrastructure and city services. Low or 
medium density residential development is appropriate at this location.

ANALYSIS OF APPLICATION CONFORMANCE WITH REVIEW CRITERIA: 
 
To accept the petition to annex and approve the annexation Ordinance, the Governing Body must make 
the following findings required in WS 15-1-402; a detailed analysis of which is below: 
 
1) An annexation of the area is for the protection of the health, safety and welfare of the 
persons residing in the area and in the city or town.  [WS 15-1-402 (a) (i)] 
 
The annexation of this property will increase the health, safety, and welfare of the occupants of 
future development once it is built. By being in City limits, the property will receive access to 
City Fire and Police and sanitation services. The property will also be connected to City water 
and sanitary sewer instead of having wells and septic systems.   
 
The application complies with this review criterion. 
 
2) The urban development of the area sought to be annexed would constitute a natural, 
geographical, economical and social part of the annexing city or town. [WS 15-1-402 (a) (ii)] 
 
This property, once developed, will constitute a natural, geographic, economic, and social part 
of the city. The contiguity to the west and the proximity to urban-style development to the 
south makes the parcel a natural geographic part of the City. The development of this property 
will benefit those who live within the City and will be a part of the social and economic fabric 
that is Cheyenne.   
 
The application complies with this review criterion. 
   
3) The area sought to be annexed is a logical and feasible addition to the annexing city or town 
and the extension of basic and other services customarily available to residents of the city or 
town shall, within reason, be available to the area proposed to be annexed. [WS 15-1-402 (a) 
(iii)] 
 
The area is a logical and feasible addition to the City due to the reasons previously listed. All City 
services normally available to residents will also be made available. Police and fire protection 
will be extended upon annexation. City water and sewer lines exist in the vicinity and could be 
extended with the development of the property (the preliminary plat associated with this 
annexation shows utility extensions). 
 
The application complies with this review criterion. 
 
4) The area sought to be annexed is contiguous with or adjacent to the annexing city or town, or 
the area meets the requirements of W.S. 15-1-407. [WS 15-1-402 (a) (iv)] 
 
The area proposed for annexation are about 18.2% contiguous, along the western edge.   
 
The application complies with this review criterion. 
 
 
 
 

UDC-21-00197
June 14, 2021
5)
If the city or town does not own or operate its own electric utility, its governing body is 
prepared to issue one (1) or more franchises as necessary to serve the annexed area pursuant 
to W.S. 15-1-410. [WS 15-1-402 (a) (v)] 
 
Utility franchises are in place to serve the annexed area.   
 
The application complies with this review criterion. 
    
6) The annexing city or town, not less than twenty (20) business days prior to the public hearing 
required by W.S. 15-1-405(a), has sent by certified mail to all landowners and affected public 
utilities within the territory a summary of the proposed annexation report as required under 
subsection (c) of this section and notice of the time, date and location of the public hearing 
required by W.S. 15-1-405(a). [WS 15-1-402 (a) (vi)] 
 
A certified mailing was made to the landowners and public utilities on May 14, 2021. The public 
hearing for this annexation is scheduled for June 14, 2021. Twenty (20) business days prior to 
the announced public hearing is May 14, 2021. The earliest confirmation of delivery was dated 
May 17, 2021. 
 
The application complies with this review criterion.   
 
CONFORMANCE WITH APPLICABLE PLANS: 
 
The project is located in an area covered by the following plans: 
 
Plan Cheyenne 
 
East Dell Range Blvd / US 30 Corridor Study 
 
Whitney Road Corridor Plan 
 
The Future Land Use Map of PlanCheyenne, as updated by Connect 2045, anticipates this area of the 
community to develop with a mix of commercial and lower-density urban residential styles of 
development. These areas are expected to develop with full access to urban services  such areas are 
best served by being within City limits. Policies in PlanCheyenne support development within the Urban 
Service Boundary and the utilization of existing infrastructure.  
 
The East Dell Range Blvd / US 30 Corridor Study and the Whitney Road Corridor Plan both anticipate that 
this area would develop with urban-style commercial and residential uses. Urban-style uses are best 
served by being within City limits and connecting to City services. 
 
STAFF REVIEW: 
 
Through the review process, the following comments were received: 
- 
Annexation should bring adjacent Dell Range Boulevard into city limits 
 
CONCLUSION: 
 
Staff is satisfied that the application meets the review criteria and therefore recommends approval of 
the annexation.   
 
The proposed annexation is a natural extension of the City, is contiguous to the current City limits, and 
services typically available to urban areas are available to this property.   

UDC-21-00197
June 14, 2021
STAFF RECOMMENDATION: 
 
Staff recommends approval of the annexation of the property. Staff recommends the inclusion of the 
adjacent portion of Dell Range right-of-way in the annexation.  
 
ATTACHMENTS:

2. Submitted Application 
3. Applicant-submitted annexation map 
4. Supporting photos

ORDINANCE NO. ____________________ 
ENTITLED: “AN ORDINANCE AMENDING THE OFFICIAL ZONING MAP OF THE CITY 
OF CHEYENNE CHANGING THE ZONING CLASSIFICATION FROM 
COUNTY MU MIXED USE AND COUNTY LR LOW DENSITY RESIDENTIAL 
TO CB COMMUNITY BUSINESS, LR LOW-DENSITY RESIDENTIAL, AND MR 
MEDIUM-DENSITY RESIDENTIAL FOR LAND  LOCATED NORTHEAST OF 
AND ADJACENT TO THE INTERSECTION OF DELL RANGE BOULEVARD, 
WEST OF WHITNEY ROAD.” 
BE IT ORDAINED BY THE GOVERNING BODY OF THE CITY OF CHEYENNE, WYOMING: 
Section 1. That the owner(s) of the property described herein have applied for a zoning map 
amendment (“zone change”) for said property in accordance with Section 2.2.1, Zoning Map Amendment,
of the Cheyenne Unified Development Code (“UDC”). 
Section 2. That on June 7, 2021, the City of Cheyenne Planning Commission conducted a public 
hearing, reviewed the proposed zone change that is the subject of this ordinance, and recommended 
approval of said zone change. 
Section 3. That the Governing Body of the City of Cheyenne finds that the zone change that is the 
subject of this ordinance meets the criteria specified in Section 2.2.1.d of the UDC. 
Section 4. That, in accordance with Section 2.2.1, Zoning Map Amendment, Section 5.1.2, Zoning 
Districts Established, and Section 5.1.3, Official Zoning Map, of the UDC, the aforementioned application 
is hereby approved and the zoning classification of the real property described as Lot 1, Block 1, 
Meadowlark Estates, 2nd Filing is hereby changed to CB Community Business; the zoning classification 
of the real property described as Lots 2-7, Block 1 and all of Block 2, Meadowlark Estates, 2nd Filing is 
hereby changed to LR Low-density Residential; and the zoning classification of the real property described 
as all of Block 3, Meadowlark Estates, 2nd Filing is hereby changed to MR Medium-density Residential. 
Section 5. That the City Engineer is hereby authorized and directed to alter and amend the Official
Zoning Map as described in Section 5.1.3 of the UDC to comply with the zone change as described in 
Section 4 above when this ordinance takes effect in accordance with Section 7 below. 
Section 6. Sections, paragraphs, sentences, clauses and phrases of this ordinance are severable. If 
any section, paragraph, sentence, clause, or phrase of this ordinance is declared unconstitutional, such 
unconstitutionality shall not affect any of the remaining sections, paragraphs, sentences, clauses, and 
phrases.
Section 7. That this ordinance shall be in full force and effect upon its approval and publication, 
and upon the official filing of the affiliated Meadowlark Estates, 2nd Filing final plat map with the County 
Clerk and Ex-Officio Register of Deeds for Laramie County, Wyoming.

STAFF REPORT 
 
FILE:
UDC-21-00198: Meadowlark Estates, 2nd Filing Zone Change 
 
REQUEST: 
A request to rezone approximately 17 acres of land from County LR and 
County MU to CB Community Business, LR Low-density Residential, and 
MR Medium-density Residential in concert with an annexation     
LOCATION: 
Northeast corner of Dell Range Boulevard and Whitney Road 
APPLICANT: 
PKT Development  Butch Tilton 
AGENT: 
Bruce Perryman, AVI, PC  
OWNER: 
Meadowlark Estates, LLC 
ZONING: 
County MU Mixed-Use, County LR Low-density Residential

Mixed-use Commercial, Urban Transition Residential 
PURPOSE: 
To give land City zoning in concert with annexation.

Seth Lloyd, Planner II - Development  
 
 
 
  
  
  
  
  Planning and Development Department 
 
(Phone) 307-637-6282 
(Fax) 307-637-6366 

UDC-21-00198
June 7, 2021
RECOMMENDED MOTION: 
 
Move to approve the rezoning of Lot 1, Block 1, Meadowlark Estates, 2nd Filing to CB Community 
Business; Lots 2-7, Block 1 and all of Block 2, Meadowlark Estates, 2nd Filing to LR Low-density 
Residential; and all of Block 3, Meadowlark Estates, 2nd Filing to MR Medium-density Residential as 
shown in the attached zone change map, noting that the project meets the review criteria for approval. 
 
APPLICABLE CITY CODE SECTION(S) AND PLANS: 
 
 
UDC 2.2.1 Zoning Map Amendment 
 
UDC Article 5 
 
PlanCheyenne 
 
BACKGROUND: 
 
This zone change application proposes changing the zoning from County MU Mixed-Use and County LR 
Low-density Residential to CB Community Business, LR Low-density Residential, and MR Medium-
density Residential for 17 acres of vacant land in concert with an annexation. The land is anticipated to be 
developed in the future with commercial and residential style uses and associated streets.    
 
SURROUNDING LAND USE AND ZONING: 
 
 
ANALYSIS OF APPLICATION CONFORMANCE WITH REVIEW CRITERIA: 
 
Should the Planning Commission wish to recommend approval of the Zoning Map Amendment, the 
Commission must make the following findings required in UDC 2.2.1.d. A detailed analysis is below: 
 
1) The application is in accordance with the development policies and physical patterns 
identified in the Comprehensive Plan.  (UDC 2.2.1.d.1) 
 
PlanCheyenne encourages growth within the Urban Service Boundary and the maximization of 
existing infrastructure. This property is within the Urban Service Boundary. Infrastructure does 
not currently exist in the area and development will require infrastructure extensions and 
improvements; however, adjacent developments may assist in the extension of infrastructure.

UDC-21-00198
June 7, 2021
The Future Land Use Designation for this property is Mixed-use Commercial Emphasis and 
Urban Transition Residential. The optimal City zoning for the future land use designation of 
Mixed-use Commercial is MUB Mixed-use Business, but commercial or higher-intensity 
residential zoning may also be appropriate. The optimal City zoning for the proposed future land 
use designation of Urban Transition Residential is LR Low-density Residential or RR Rural 
Residential, but slightly higher-intensity or lower-intensity residential zoning may also be 
appropriate, especially on the edges of the area designated. 
 
Based on the future land use designations, the proposed LR zone is the optimal zone for areas 
proposed to be zoned LR. While MUB is the optimal zone for the area designated Mixed-use 
Commercial, the CB zone is an acceptable alternative which would allow commercial 
development at the intersection. The area proposed to be zoned MR serves as a transition 
between the mixed-use area to the south and the lower-intensity residential areas to the north.  
 
The proposed zone change complies with review criterion 1. 
 
2) The application is compatible with nearby property, specifically considering existing zoning, 
the Comprehensive Plan, and any other official plans and policies created under the guidance 
of that plan for these areas; (UDC 2.2.1.d.2) 
 
To the north and east, the existing zoning is County LR and designated Urban Transition 
Residential by the comprehensive plan. The area of this re-zoning adjacent to this land is 
proposed to be zoned LR, which is the City zone most analogous to that County zone and 
considered the most appropriate City zone for areas and designated Urban Transition 
Residential.  
 
The portion of this zone change proposed to be zoned MR is adjacent to areas zoned MU and NB 
in the County and designated Mixed-use Commercial, across a major transportation route. The 
MR zone is a lower-intensity zone than these adjacent zones, and would not have an undue 
influence on the surrounding parcels to the south. 
 
The area proposed to be zoned CB is at the intersection of two major transportation routes. The 
property to the south is zoned MU in the County and designated Mixed-use Commercial. The 
property to the west is zoned AG in the City and also designated Mixed-use Commercial. The AG 
zone is a holding zone for future development  the area zoned AG adjacent to the area 
proposed to be zoned CB is anticipated to be zoned commercial use in the near future (likely CB 
zoning). While CB zoning is generally considered a higher-intensity zone than MU, the 
commercial expectation of the future land use designation and the location at a major 
intersection means CB zoning is compatible with nearby property. 
 
The proposed zone change for the subject property complies with review criterion 2. 
   
3) The suitability of the subject property for development according to the standards and uses of 
the existing zoning district, compared to the suitability of the subject property for 
development according to the standards and uses of the proposed zoning district (UDC 
2.2.1.d.3) 
  
The current County LR zone allows lower-density, urban residential style uses, and the County 
MU zone allows a variety of commercial and residential uses  but in order to develop with lots 
any smaller than 5 acres, connections to a central sanitary sewer system is required. There is no 

UDC-21-00198
June 7, 2021
sanitary sewer system available in this area of the County outside of the Board of Public Utilities. 
The BOPU generally only services land within City limits (special exceptions are required to 
service unannexed land). The requirement for sanitary sewer or minimum lot sizes for septic 
system provision limits development options. 
 
As the property is proposed to be annexed, City zoning is required. The proposed City LR zone is 
the zone most similar to the current County LR zone, and provides a similar range of 
development options, but by being part of the City, BOPU sewer would be anticipated to be 
extended to the parcel, allowing functional LR-style development. 
 
The MR zone allows a broader mix of residential uses than the current County LR zone. The CB 
zone allows a range of commercial uses, but limits residential development.    
   
The proposed zone change complies with review criterion 3. 
 
4) The ability of the subject property and surrounding vicinity to be developed according to the 
Subdivision Standards in Article 4 considering the proposed zoning (UDC 2.2.1.d.4) 
 
The property is constrained by topography, adjacent major transportation routes, and previous 
development. The ability to create blocks and connections that meet the standards of Article 4 
would be limited, no matter what City zoning was proposed. Also, due to the existing patterns, 
the ability of this land to meet optimal intersection spacing or block arrangement may be 
limited. The property is able to meet other standards of Article 4, but specific application of 
these standards to this land is reviewed and analyzed with the platting action. A concurrent 
Preliminary Plat is proposed with this zone change application.  
 
The proposed zone change for the subject property complies with review criterion 4. 
 
5) The ability of the application to support the Intent and Applicability statements of the 
proposed zoning district and achieve the Design Objectives of any specific standards. (UDC 
2.2.1.d.5) 
 
The CB zone is intended for retail, employment, service and civic uses that may serve a number 
of residential neighborhoods. Typically, areas zoned CB are expected to be more automobile-
oriented and serve the region. It is typically expected in areas designated as activity centers or 
Community Business by the comprehensive plan. The future land use map designates the 
intersection of Christensen and US 30 as an activity center, and the area around the intersection 
of Whitney and US 30 as Community Business. Commercial development at this intersection is 
anticipated by the properties mixed-use future land use designation, and any commercial 
development at this intersection would likely have a regional service area (including the 
surrounding rural and low-to-medium density residential developments). 
 
The LR zone is intended for a mix of lower density residential development with access to public 
infrastructure and city services. LR zoning is anticipated in areas designated Urban Transition 
Residential and should be less than a mile from commercial development. This area is 
designated Urban Transition Residential and is practically adjacent to current and future 
commercial development. 
 
The MR zone is intended for a mix of residential densities and dwelling types with access to 
public infrastructure and city services. MR zoning is anticipated in areas designated Urban 

UDC-21-00198
June 7, 2021
Residential and should be less than a half mile from commercial development. While this area is 
not designated Urban Residential, the area proposed to be zoned MR provides a transition from 
the area zoned LR and nearby commercial or mixed-use zoning. 
 
General commercial design standards apply to the CB zone. The area zoned CB would be 
required to provide a landscape buffer to the adjacent area zoned LR. The area zoned CB is not 
anticipated to have issues meeting the design standards. 
 
Any residential development within the City is expected to meet the general residential design 
standards. These standards apply to the LR and MR zones, and these zone districts have no 
zone-specific standards. 
 
The proposed zone change for the subject property complies with review criterion 5. 
    
6) The ability of all potential future development to meet all applicable standards for 
development in the proposed zoning district, including other development options if any 
specific development proposed in association with the application is not executed after 
approval. (UDC 2.2.1.d.6) 
 
The applicant is seeking the proposed zone change in concert with an annexation. The proposed 
HI and LI zoning does not have zone-specific design standards beyond setback requirements, 
and the parcels proposed to be zoned LI and HI are large enough to allow development to meet 
the setback requirements. The proposed AG zoning is the zone most similar to the existing 
zoning on the property and on surrounding County properties. Development on the property 
zoned AG would require a future rezoning. 
 
A specific development pattern is proposed on the land to be zoned, and the zoning amendment 
is dependent on alignment with the associated plat. Should the specific plat proposed for the 
land not be recorded after the zone change is approved, a separate zoning action should be 
pursued. Should the plat be recorded, but subsequently not developed, the areas zoned LR and 
MR could be replatted to accommodate different development patterns which would still be 
appropriate at this location. 
 
There is no specific development proposed for the parcel to be zoned CB, and the CB-zoned 
parcel would have multiple development options.  
 
The proposed zone change complies with review criterion 6. 
 
7) The review and recommendations of other reviewing bodies. (UDC 2.2.1.d.7) 
 
No reviewing agencies provided recommendations regarding the proposed zone change.  
 
The proposed zone change for the subject property complies with review criterion 7. 
 
 
 
 
 
 

UDC-21-00198
June 7, 2021
8)
Whether the application will harm the public health, safety, or general welfare. (UDC 
2.2.1.d.8) 
 
City staff is satisfied that this proposed zone change would not adversely affect the public 
health, safety, or general welfare. 
 
The proposed zone change for the subject property complies with review criterion 8. 
 
CONFORMANCE WITH APPLICABLE PLANS: 
 
The project is located in an area covered by the following plans: 
 
PlanCheyenne 
 
East Dell Range Boulevard / US 30 Corridor Study 
 
Whitney Road Corridor Plan 
Conformance with PlanCheyenne is covered by review criterion 1.     
 
Both the East Dell Range Boulevard / US 30 Corridor Study and the Whitney Road Corridor Plan both 
anticipate the development of this area with urban-style uses. 
 
 
STAFF REVIEW: 
 
Through the review process, comments were received regarding the following issues: 
1. Alignment of information shown on the zone change map and preliminary plat map. 
 
CONCLUSION: 
 
Staff is satisfied that the application meets the review criteria set forth in UDC 2.2.1.(d).  The zoning map 
amendment application is proposed in concert with an annexation. The CB, LR, and MR zones are 
appropriate based on the future land use designation for the subject property. Surrounding zoning 
includes lower-density residential and commercial zoning. Surrounding development patterns include 
rural residential and commercial development. The proposed zone change would not have an undue 
influence on the surrounding area. 
 
ALTERNATIVES: 
 
1. Recommend approval of the zoning amendment as submitted by the applicant (Staff 
Recommendation).  
2. Recommend approval of the zoning amendment subject to conditions or modifications.  
3. Recommend denial of the zoning amendment based on findings of denial. The Planning 
Commission must conclude that the project does not meet all the review criteria. As part of the 
motion, findings for denial must be stated. 
4. Postpone the zoning amendment until issues identified during the meeting can be resolved. 
 
General Information Regarding Alternatives: 
 
If 
Commission shall cite specific reasons (based on the review criteria) for approval or denial. 
 

UDC-21-00198
June 7, 2021
STAFF RECOMMENDATION: 
 
Staff recommends approval of the rezoning of the property from County MU and County LR to CB, LR 
and MR. 
 
ATTACHMENTS: 
1. Aerial, Future Land Use Plan, and Current Zoning Map 
2. Master Application 
3. Applicant Provided Zone Change Map

(Expedited Request)

UDC-21-00102
May 3, 2021
RECOMMENDED MOTION: 
 
Move to approve the Saddle Ridge 16th Filing 
conditions, noting that as conditioned, the project meets the review criteria for approval; and that a 
final plat addressing these conditions of approval be prepared and presented to the Governing Body. 
 
APPLICABLE CITY CODE SECTION(S) AND PLANS: 
 
 
UDC 2.1.2 Preliminary Plat Approval  
 
UDC 2.1.3 Final Plat Approval  
 
UDC Article 4 Subdivisions 
 
UDC Article 5 Zoning Regulations 
 
PlanCheyenne 
 
BACKGROUND: 
 
The applicant is requesting approval of a preliminary plat for a 4.6 acre tract of land to create 26 lots for 
residential development, with associated streets and open space. The land is currently undeveloped in the 
County and concurrent zone change and annexation applications have been submitted. The land has never 
been previously platted. However, a regional Preliminary Plat in 2015 (Saddle Ridge 8th/9th Filing) proposed a 
street layout and development pattern for about 140 acres, of which this plat would be a portion. The 
currently-proposed plat reconfigures this section of the larger Preliminary Plat, but generally is consistent 
with the overall development program and network proposed by the original Preliminary Plat. 
 
SURROUNDING LAND USE AND ZONING: 
 
 
 
The property is currently zoned County A-1 Agricultural and Rural Residential, and is proposed to be 
zoned MR Medium-density Residential. The MR zone is intended for a mix of medium-density residential 
uses.

UDC-21-00102
May 3, 2021
ANALYSIS OF APPLICATION CONFORMANCE WITH REVIEW CRITERIA: 
 
Should the Planning Commission wish to recommend approval of the Expedited Plat, the Commission 
must make the following findings required in UDC 2.1.2.d and UDC 2.1.3.d a detailed analysis is below: 
 
1) The application is in accordance with the development policies and physical patterns 
identified in the Comprehensive Plan. (UDC 2.1.2.d.1) 
 
On the future land use map found in PlanCheyenne, the subject land and surrounding area has 
been designated as Urban Residential. The Urban Residential designation encourages a range of 
more intensive residential uses.  
 
Foundation 1 of PlanCheyenne encourages community growth. This development is in response 
to market demands and is within the Urban Service Boundary. The development patterns 
proposed by this plat are compatible with the designations found on the future land use map. 
 
Foundation 2 of PlanCheyenne encourages new residential development to be compatible and 
connected with existing residential neighborhoods. The proposed plat ties into the adjacent 
neighborhoods and is consistent with the overall development program proposed by a 
previously-approved regional preliminary plat.   
 
Foundation 7 of PlanCheyenne support the use of existing infrastructure and timely provision of 
services (including emergency and education services). Large developments on the edge of the 
community can create issues with providing services in an effective and timely fashion. 
Development of this area of the community creates the need to improve street and emergency 
service infrastructure. 
 
The application complies with this review criterion.   
 
2) The application reflects sound planning and urban design principles with respect to existing 
and potential future development in the vicinity, including consideration of current and future 
streets, open spaces, blocks and lots, and public utilities or facilities. (UDC 2.1.2.d.2) 
 
This plat proposes creating 26 lots for residential development and one lot for open space. The 
plat also dedicates streets. The proposed layout is a different configuration from the original 
preliminary plat, but the exterior boundaries of the plat are substantially similar for this section 
and the plat is consistent with the previously-approved preliminary plat. The proposed plat 
would not have an undue influence on surrounding properties. 
 
The application complies with this review criterion.   
   
3) The division of land meets the purposes, intent, and all applicable standards in Article 4. (UDC 
2.1.2.d.3) 
 
Transportation Network: 
This plat is occurring within the area of a previously-approved preliminary plat which proposed a 
street network. The layout of this plat is consistent with the larger, previously-approved 
network. This project is proposing modifications to the approved Preliminary Plat on 
intersections and layout, hence the need for a new preliminary plat for this area. 
 

UDC-21-00102
May 3, 2021
Street section standards are also a part of Article 4, and the proposed east-west alley / 
hammerhead does not meet street standards. The other streets proposed by this subdivision 
meet street section standards. Staff has conditioned this project (Condition 1.a.) to require the 
alley shown on the plat be renamed to Three Hearts Trail and the applicant obtain a subdivision 
standard waiver for local street with and design prior to approval of the final plat.    
 
Community Facility Fees: 
For fees based on acreage, the area of the plat is pro-rated to the nearest tenth of an acre. The 
required fees for the 3.2 acres of non-exempt land in this plat are as follows: 
Land Acquisition Fee  
 
 
 
$1,120 ($350 per acre) 
Infrastructure Fee   
 
 
 
$1,120 ($350 per acre) 
Total Land Acquisition & Infrastructure Fees 
$2,240.00 
Per UDC Section 4.2.5, Table 4-2, the Land Acquisition & Infrastructure fee are payable at 
the time of the signing of the final plat.  
Total Fees due at signing of the plat: 
 
$2,240.00 
 
Per UDC Section 4.2.5, Table 4-2, Enhancement Fees are payable at the issuance of a 
building permit for each structure. 
Residential Enhancement Fee 
$400 per dwelling unit 
Commercial Enhancement Fee 
$1,100 per acre 
 
Open Space: 
Open space is required at 750sqft per unit or 8% of the gross development area, whichever is 
less. Lot 11, Block 3 is proposed as an open space parcel. This is part of the larger open space 
system proposed by the general layout of Saddle Ridge as put forward by the approved Saddle 
Ridge 8th/9th Preliminary Plat.  
 
Required Improvements: 
This appears to be able to provide all required utilities to the lots within this development; 
however, the specific proposed layout of BOPU utilities is not shown. The BOPU asked for the 
proposed layout to be provided to ascertain if the layout was appropriate with needed 
easements, but this has not yet been provided. Staff has conditioned this plat (Condition 2.a). 
 
Developments are required to provide improvements adjacent to their development.  At this 
time Pershing Boulevard is improved to a rural road standard.   Improvement of the road to 
urban standards is premature at this time due to the remoteness of this plat. Staff has 
recommended a condition that prior to final plat approval by the Governing Body the developer 
enter into an agreement for delayed improvements to Pershing Boulevard for their 
proportionate share of roadway improvements.    
 
Greenway: 
In conjunction with the Christensen Road project the City 
between Dell Range Boulevard and Commerce Circle. Also included in the project was the 
addition of a Greenway on the north side of Pershing Boulevard extending from Christensen 
Road extending west to the edge of this development.   Staff has recommended a condition 
to connect to Dixon Drive, 
design be provided with subsequent Engineering Plan Reviews, and the Greenway be 
constructed in conjunction with the subdivision.    
 
As conditioned, this application complies with this review criterion.   

UDC-21-00102
May 3, 2021
4)
Any phasing proposed in the application is clearly indicated and demonstrates a logical and 
well planned development pattern. This may include consideration of amenities and public 
facilities of the subdivision within each phase compared to the anticipated timing or the 
likelihood of future phases being constructed as planned. (UDC 2.1.2.d.4) 
 
No phasing is proposed with this project. 
 
The application complies with this review criterion.   
 
5) The impacts identified in specific studies or technical reports that were prepared or requested 
to be prepared in association with the application, and whether the findings and 
recommendations of these reports, or any modifications or conditions on approval of the 
application, can mitigate these impacts. (UDC 2.1.2.d.5) 
 
A drainage study and an addendum to a previously-submitted traffic study were submitted with 
this application. 
 
Drainage:  The site generally slopes from the northwest toward the southeast. The site itself is 
on a rise. The report submitted meets requirements for this stage of development. 
 
Traffic:  The Traffic Study addendum was completed for a previous project and there were no 
modifications made for this specific proposal. The City traffic engineer has asked for a letter to 
update the traffic study with specifics to this proposal. The applicant has indicated they are in 
consultation with their traffic engineer; however, nothing has yet been submitted to City Staff 
(see condition 1.d).   
 
The application complies with this review criterion with conditions. 
  
6) Lots proposed for development shall be buildable lots according to the existing or proposed 
zoning district standards, result in design and development patterns that meet the Intent and 
Applicability statements of the existing or proposed zoning district, and not aggravate 
nonconforming setbacks or currently adopted building and fire code access and separation 
requirements. (UDC 2.1.2.d.6) 
 
The proposed application occurs in concert with a proposal to zone the land MR Medium-
density Residential. All lots which are proposed to be buildable meet the dimensional standards 
of the MR zone with the following caveats:  
- 
Lots on Block 3 are too small to meet the standards for single-family home 
development, these lots will be required to be built with duplexes. 
- 
Some of the Lots along Block 3 have frontage on a public alley / hammerhead 
instead of a public street. While the frontage meets code standards, the street 
fronted upon does not. 
 
The MR zone is intended to develop with a variety of residential uses, and this plat includes both 
lots intended for single-family and duplex style residential development. 
 
The application complies with this review criterion.   
 
 

UDC-21-00102
May 3, 2021
7)
All parcels not proposed as buildable lots and which are not dedicated to and accepted by the 
City shall have a clearly defined entity responsible for ongoing maintenance.  (UDC 2.1.2.d.7) 
 
This plat proposes 26 buildable lots and 1 unbuildable open space lot. The open space lot is 
proposed to be given to the City for ownership and maintenance and the development of a 
Greenway corridor.  
 
The application complies with this review criterion.   
 
8) The application will not create any undue influence on existing or potential development for 
adjacent property considering the existing zoning and Comprehensive Plan policies for that 
property.  (UDC 2.1.2.d.8) 
 
Properties adjacent to this plat to the east are zoned MR and P within City limits and have a 
similar development pattern to the pattern proposed by this plat. The areas to the north and 
east are currently undeveloped in the County, but are part of the Saddle Ridge 8th/9th 
Preliminary Plat which identified a similar development pattern throughout the area. The area 
to the south across Pershing is largely developed with a rural residential pattern outside of City 
limits. The entire area is designated Urban Residential by the Future Land Use Map, which 
encourages a mix of urban-style residential development patterns. The proposed plat includes 
the extension of a street which will allow future development to the north and east. This plat 
would not have any undue influence on surrounding properties.  
 
The application complies with this review criterion.   
   
9) The application is not harmful to the public health, safety, and welfare. (UDC 2.1.2.d.9) 
 
Street Naming: 
Naming and addressing properties for emergency services is necessary. The substandard street / 
alley / hammerhead is unnamed and addressing the parcels of Block 3 is a public safety concern. 
Staff has recommended a condition of preliminary plat approval to require naming of the alley 
as Three Hearts Trail (Condition 2.b).  
 
Utilities: 
The adequate provision of water and sewer is a public health concern. Staff is satisfied the 
proposed plat would not otherwise adversely affect the public health, safety, or general welfare 
(Condition 2.a).   
 
Public Safety: 
This development represents a continued expansion and an increase in public safety service in 
the City.   Recent preliminary plats have been required to contribute a monetary amount on a 
per unit basis for public safety fees to be placed in the Development Impact Fund earmarked for 
 Staff has recommended a 
(Condition 1.c) that the developer enter into a development agreement for this public safety 
prior to final plat approval.  This dollar amount utilized in $756 per unit based on January 2021 
costs.   The City will utilize that base fee and adjust for inflation utilizing the  construction cost 
index for twenty (20) cities, as issued by the current Engineering News Record which is similarly 
used for calculating infrastructure reimbursements pursuant to Cheyenne Municipal Code 
subsection 1.16.070.b at the time the plat is scheduled for the Governing Body.  Based on 
January 2021 numbers, the fee for 26 units is $19,656.  When adjusted for 3.78% inflation 

UDC-21-00102
May 3, 2021
through May 2021, the estimated total public safety fees is $20,398.21 ($19,656 * 03.78%).  This 
number will be finalized in the agreement for the time the plat is scheduled for Governing Body 
consideration. 
 
Solid Waste and Green Waste Collection 
Creation of three four lots along a dead-end narrow road with no turnaround can pose a 
collection problem for solid waste, recycling, and green waste trucks.  Staff has recommended a 
note on the plat that solid waste, recycling, and green waste services are not guaranteed to be 
provided curbside in the event pick-up cannot safely occur  (Condition 2.c.).  A majority of the 
locations. 
 
As conditioned, the application complies with this review criterion with conditions. 
 
10) Remainder lot(s) shall be clearly identified on the preliminary plat and shall not be developed 
or proposed for development for purposes.  Remainder lot(s) shall be noted and described on 
all plats either graphically or by legal description. (UDC 2.1.2.d.10) 
 
Remainder lots are not proposed with this plat. 
 
The application complies with this review criterion.   
 
11) The layout and design of the final plat is in substantial compliance with the approved 
preliminary plat considering the number of lots or parcels, street and block layout, and access, 
and any deviations in the final plat brings the application in further compliance with the 
Comprehensive Plan. (UDC 2.1.3.d.1) 
 
The Final Plat submitted with the application is substantially similar to the Preliminary Plat 
submitted. 
 
The application complies with this review criterion.   
 
12) The application meets any conditions or modifications of the approved preliminary plat. (UDC 
2.1.3.d.2) 
 
Through the review process and application of the Preliminary Plat, Staff has recommended 
conditions to the plat. These conditions are summarized in the staff recommendation. While this 
condition does not pose a significant engineering or planning issue, it should be addressed prior 
 Plat.  
 
The application complies with this review criterion, with conditions.   
   
13) The application meets the purposes, intent, and applicable standards of Article 4. (UDC 
2.1.3.d.3) 
 
As a concurrent Preliminary and Final Plat application, this Final Plat review criterion is covered 
by the equivalent Preliminary Plat review criterion (subsection 3 above). 
 
The application complies with this review criterion.   
 

UDC-21-00102
May 3, 2021

PlanCheyenne

CONCLUSION: 
 
Staff is satisfied that the application largely meets the review criteria and therefore recommends 
approval of the expedited plat application as submitted by the applicant, with conditions.  
 
The area this plat is proposed in is anticipated to develop as a largely residential neighborhood, based 
on both existing development and the patterns proposed by the comprehensive plan. The proposed plat 
is consistent with this development pattern.

WHEREAS, Arp and Hammond Hardware Company, Lummis Livestock 
Company, LLC, and the Wyoming Department of Transportation, has subdivided said land 
in accordance with the statutes in such cases made and provided; and 
WHEREAS, Arp and Hammond Hardware Company, Lummis Livestock

WHEREAS, the above described subdivision plat has been presented to the City

NOW, THEREFORE BE IT RESOLVED BY THE GOVERNING BODY OF

STAFF REPORT 
 
 
FILE:

REQUEST:

of Transportation

Brad Emmons, AVI, PC

County A-2 Agricultural

Industrial

6 
TOTAL AREA: 
362.4 acres

Seth Lloyd, Planner II  Development 
 
  
  
  
  
  Planning and Development Department 
 
(Phone) 307-637-6282 
(Fax) 307-637-6366 

UDC-21-00127
May 3, 2021
RECOMMENDED MOTION: 
 
Move to recommend the Governing Body approve the Bison Business Park, 2nd Filing Final Plat, subject 
to S
s, noting that as conditioned, the project meets the review criteria for 
approval; and that a final plat addressing these conditions of approval be prepared and presented to the 
Governing Body. 
 
APPLICABLE CITY CODE SECTION(S) AND PLANS: 
 
 
UDC 2.1.3 Final Plat  
 
UDC Article 4 Subdivisions 
 
Plan Cheyenne 
 
 
BACKGROUND: 
 
This is a request to final plat 6 lots, with associated public rights-of-way. The lots were preliminary 
platted as part of the Bison Business Park preliminary plat which was approved by the Governing Body 
on February 8, 2021. Pursuant to UDC 2.1.2.c.6.a preliminary plats remain active 18 months after their 
approval or since the last final plat was approved within the preliminary plat.   
 
Concurrent applications for an annexation and a zone change (to LI, HI, AG, and P) have also been submitted.    
 
ANALYSIS OF APPLICATION CONFORMANCE WITH REVIEW CRITERIA: 
 
Should the Planning Commission wish to recommend approval of final plat, the Commission must make 
the following findings required in UDC 2.1.3.d a detailed analysis is below. The purpose of a final plat 
review is to ensure compliance with the approved preliminary plat and its conditions of approval.   
 
1) The layout and design of the final plat is in substantial compliance with the approved 
preliminary plat considering the number of lots or parcels, street and block layout, and access, 
and any deviations in the final plat brings the application in further compliance with the 
Comprehensive Plan. (UDC 2.1.3.d.1) 
 
The layout of the Final Plat is substantively compliant with the approved Preliminary Plat in 
layout. Lots and streets are re-aligned, but are substantially shown in the same locations and are 
substantially the same in area (when considering the size and scope of the development).  
 
Loving Trail no longer continues south of Wisent, however the Preliminary Plat had Loving Trail 
terminating in a cul-de-sac, so no network connections are lost by the removal of this section of 
right-of-way. The two lots on either side of the Loving Trail cul-de-sac have been consolidated 
into one lot. Lot 2, Block 4 had access to Loving Trail, but no longer has such access. This lot 
would be required to access South Greeley Highway or Chalk Bluff Road. Due to the access 
limitations associated with major transportation corridors, it is likely that access would be 
limited to Chalk Bluff Road. Staff recommends a condition that a note be added to the plat 
preventing access to South Greeley Highway for this lot. 
 
The WYDOT parcel was not part of the Preliminary Plat; however, this parcel is bounded on 
three sides by the Preliminary Plat and on the 4th side by the South Greeley Highway right-of-
way. The layout of this parcel was therefore effectively defined by the Preliminary Plat and 

UDC-21-00127
May 3, 2021
including it within this plat allows for cleaner and clearer delineation of the parcel. Including the 
parcel in the plat is more of a 'housekeeping' action than a true deviation from the original plat. 
 
The application complies with this review criterion, as conditioned.   
 
2) The application meets any conditions or modifications of the approved preliminary plat. (UDC 
2.1.3.d.2) 
 
The preliminary plat was approved on January 4th 2021 by the Planning Commission, and the 
first Final Plat associated with the Preliminary Plat was approved February 8, 2021  both 
subject to the following condition: 
1. Prior to recording of the final plat, Land Acquisition and Infrastructure Fees totaling 
$180,740 shall be paid to the City. 
The Final Plat which this condition referenced has been filed and the condition has been 
fulfilled. The condition is not applicable to this Final Plat application. 
 
The application complies with this review criterion.   
   
3) The application meets the purposes, intent, and applicable standards of Article 4. (UDC 
2.1.3.d.3) 
 
Transportation Network 
Concepts regarding block layout, street design, and access were evaluated with the 
Preliminary Plat and no changes to the larger network are proposed with the Final Plat 
submittal. The removal of the Loving Trail cul-de-sac does not remove or modify any 
network connections. 
 
Community Facilities and Fees 
Fee analysis regarding fees dues, exemptions, and credits were evaluated with the 
Preliminary Plat; however due to the phased nature of this project, fees become due at 
different times. Lands reserved for right-of-way are exempt from paying fees. Lands used by 
governmental agencies (as Lot 1, Block 1) are also exempt. Lands zoned AG are exempt until 
rezoned out of the AG zone. Plat fees are therefore only applicable to the parcels proposed 
to be zoned LI and HI. For fees based on acreage, the area of the plat is pro-rated to the 
nearest tenth of an acre. The required fees for the 122.1 acres of non-exempt land in this 
plat are as follows: 
Land Acquisition Fee  
 
 
 
$42,735 ($350 per acre) 
Infrastructure Fee   
 
 
 
$42,735 ($350 per acre) 
Total Land Acquisition & Infrastructure Fees 
$85,470.00 
Per UDC Section 4.2.5, Table 4-2, the Land Acquisition & Infrastructure fee are payable at 
the time of the signing of the final plat.  
 
Per UDC Section 4.2.5, Table 4-2, Enhancement Fees are payable at the issuance of a 
building permit for each structure.  The Commercial Enhancement Fee is $1,100 per acre. 
  
Total Fees due at signing of the plat: 
 $85,470.00 
 
Open Space Systems 
Open Space is not required for areas designated Industrial by the Future Land Use Map and 
the industrial and agricultural zones have no open space requirements.  

UDC-21-00127
May 3, 2021
Required Improvements and Engineering Specifications
This proposed subdivision appears to be able to provide all required utilities to the 
properties within this development. The Board of Public Utilities is requesting easements to 
allow the effective extension of water and sewer. 
 
The application complies with this review criterion, as conditioned.   
 
STAFF REVIEW: 
 
Through the review process, comments were received regarding the following issues: 
1. Board of Public Utilities (BOPU) easements.  
2. Drainage design needed with site development and street construction. 
3. Potential access for Lot 2, Block 4. 
4. The WYDOT ownership of a portion of the plat. 
5. Timing the construction of the public streets. 
6. Potential pedestrian or multi-use path through the development. 
7. Consistency with the approved Preliminary Plat.  
 
The foregoing comments resulted in the following recommended conditions for approval: 
1. Add Board of Public Utilities (BOPU) easements to Lot 2, Block 4 to the Final Plat. 
2. Add a note to the plat limiting access to Lot 2, Block 4 to Chalk Bluff Road. 
3. Add a signature block for the Wyoming Department of Transportation to sign as an owner. 
 
CONCLUSION: 
 
Staff is satisfied that the application meets the review criteria set forth in UDC 2.1.3.(d) for Final Plat 
approval. The Final Plat is consistent with the approved Preliminary Plat. Staff recommends approval of 
the Final Plat with the following conditions: 
1. Add Board of Public Utilities (BOPU) easements to Lot 2, Block 4 to the Final Plat. 
2. Add a note to the plat limiting access to Lot 2, Block 4 to Chalk Bluff Road. 
3. Add a signature block for the Wyoming Department of Transportation to sign as an owner. 
4. Correct the vicinity map to match the Final Plat boundary. 
 
ALTERNATIVES: 
 
1. Recommend approval of the project as submitted by the applicant. 
2. Recommend that the application be approved with specific conditions or modifications, and that 
a final plat addressing the conditions of approval be prepared and presented to the Governing 
Body. (Staff Recommendation) 
3. Recommend that the application be denied based on findings of denial. The Planning 
Commission must conclude that the project does not meet all the review criteria. As part of the 
motion, findings for denial must be stated. 
4. Postpone the project until issues identified during the meeting can be resolved. 
 
General Information Regarding Options 
 
The Commission may only condition the final plat in select circumstances.  Conditions or modifications 
shall be to better meet standards and review criteria; or to address situations created by or aggravated 
by the proposed application, and which conditions are roughly proportional to the impacts of the 
proposed development.  New conditions shall not be added. 

UDC-21-00127
May 3, 2021
STAFF RECOMMENDATION: 
 
Staff recommends that the Planning Commission recommend the Governing Body approve the Bison 
Business Park, 2nd Filing Final Plat with the following conditions: 
1. Add Board of Public Utilities (BOPU) easements to Lot 2, Block 4 to the Final Plat. 
2. Add a note to the plat limiting access to Lot 2, Block 4 to Chalk Bluff Road. 
3. Add a signature block for the Wyoming Department of Transportation to sign as an owner. 
4. Correct the vicinity map to match the Final Plat boundary. 
 
ATTACHMENTS: 
1. Aerial, Zoning, and Future Land Use Map 
2. Application 
3. Approved preliminary plat  
4. Final Plat

RESOLUTION NO.________
ENTITLED: “A RESOLUTION AMENDING RESOLUTION NOS. 5934 AND 5998
PERTAINING TO ADOPTION OF A REVISED CHEYENNE-LARAMIE
COUNTY BOARD OF HEALTH FEE SCHEDULE FOR INSPECTION
SERVICES, PERMIT FEES, AND OTHER VARIOUS SERVICES.”
WHEREAS, Wyo. Stat. § 35-1-305(a) authorizes local boards of health to fix and charge
reasonable fees for services; and
WHEREAS, the Cheyenne-Laramie County Board ofHealth currently provides inspection
services for food service operations, body art, childcare facilities, campgrounds, small wastewater
systems, swimming pools/spas, massage establishments and therapists, non-hazardous liquid waste
haulers, rural property; and
WHEREAS, the Cheyenne-Laramie County Board of Health wishes to amend its health fee
schedule for inspection services, permit fees, and other various services as previously adopted by
City Resolution No. 5934, approved by the Governing Body on June 11,2018, and City Resolution
No. 5998, approved by the Governing Body on June 24, 2019, to revise the health fee schedule for
inspection services, permit fees, and other various services provided by the Cheyenne-Laramie
County Board of Health, which health fee schedule is set forth in Exhibit A attached to this
resolution and incorporated by reference herein; and
WHEREAS, the public hearing required by Wyo. Stat. § 35-1-305(c) was conducted on May
18, 2021 by the Cheyenne-Laramie County Board of Health; and
WHEREAS, prior to the fees being assessed, the health fee schedule must be adopted by the
City of Cheyenne and Laramie County Board of Commissioners for its respective jurisdictions.
NOW, THEREFORE, BE IT RESOLVED BY THE GOVERNING BODY OF THE
CITY OF CHEYENNE, WYOMING, that the revised health fee schedule for inspection services,
permit fees, and other various services provided by the Cheyenne-Laramie County Board ofHealth,
which health fee schedule is set forth in Exhibit A, is hereby adopted.
PRESENTED, READ, AND ADOPTED this
day of
-
, 2021
Patrick Collins, Mayor

RESOLUTION ADOPTING CHANGES TO FEES CHARGED FOR VARIOUS
SERVICES AND INSPECTIONS PURSUANT TO W.S. 35-1-305
WHEREAS, W.S. §35-1-305(a), authorizes the Cheyenne-Laramie County
Board of Health to impose and modify fees charged for various services and inspections
provided and performed by the Cheyenne-Laramie County Health Department in its
mission for the prevention of disease and the promotion of health within Laramie County
Wyoming with approval of the Laramie County Commissioners and the City of
Cheyenne;
WHEREAS, W.S. §16-3-103, requires at least 45 days public notice and a
reasonable opportunity for public comment before the Cheyenne-Laramie County Board
of Health to adopt, amend, or repeal rules and regulations;
WHEREAS, the Cheyenne-Laramie County Board of Health has complied with
the requirements of W.S. § 16-3-103(a);
NOW THEREFORE BE IT RESOLVED BY THE CHEYENNE-LARAMIE
COUNTY BOARD OF HEALTH, that the Cheyenne-Laramie County Board of Health
enacts, effective July 1, 2021, changes to fees charged by the Cheyenne-Laramie County
Health Department as reflected in the attached fee structure and repeals previously
enacted fees for the same services and inspections.

FOR IMMEDIATE RELEASE
October 16, 2018
Contact: Amy Bittner, Senior Economist
ECONOMIC ANALYSIS DIVISION
Wyoming Cost of Living Index for the Second Quarter of 2018
CHEYENNE
— The State of Wyoming, Economic Analysis Division reported that Wyoming
experienced a 3.3 percent increase in annual (All Items) inflation from the second quarter of
2017 to the second quarter of2018. Inflation is measured over six consumer categories in the
Wyoming Cost of Living Index (WCLI). Statewide annual inflation rates by consumer category
for the second quarter of 2018: Transportation (8.7%), Apparel (6.6%), Medical (3.6%), Housing
(2.1%), Food (2.1%), and Recreation & Personal Care (-0.3%). Higher gasoline prices
contributed to the year-over-year Transportation inflation. Consumers saw higher prices at the
pump due to increased oil prices. During this same time, the U.S. Consumer Price Index (U.S.
CPI) reported inflation of 2.9 percent (June 2017 to June 2018).
Inflation figures are also available for five regions in the State. For the second quarter of 2018,
the Northeast region recorded the highest annual increase (4.1%) followed by the Central (3.6%),
Southeast (3.2%), Northwest (3.2%), and Southwest (1.8%). Inflation measures year-over-year
prices changes. The inflation figures compare the second quarter of 2018 to the second quarter
of 2017. An economic downturn in Wyoming began in early 2015. At this time, the State
experienced heavy employment losses and reductions in revenue due to decreased mineral
extraction activities.
Wyoming lost one percent of its population from July 1, 2016 to July 1,
2017. It was not until the end of 2016 that things started to pick up in the State’s mining industry
(including oil and gas extraction). Wyoming’s total employment in the second quarter of 2018
increased by 1.4 percent from one year earlier. This growth was the largest year-over-year
increase since the fourth quarter of 2014.
The Wyoming Cost of Living Index (WCLI) consists of two parts, Inflation, which measures
year-over-year change (annual) and the Comparative Cost of Living Index which compares
each county’s cost of living in one period (not over time) to the statewide average. The inflation
figures for the second quarter of 2018 represent the percent change in the price level of a
standard basket of selected consumer items priced in the second quarter of 2018, compared with
the price level of the same goods recorded one year ago (second quarter of 2017). The
Comparative Cost of Living Index represents each countys price level compared to the statewide
average (100) during a single time period, in this case, the second quarter of 2018.
For the second quarter of 2018, the Comparative Cost of Living Index ranked Teton (158) as the
county with the highest All Items value, which indicates that the cost of living in Teton County

was estimated 58 percent higher than the statewide average in the quarter. Sublette County
ranked second in the All Items Comparative Cost of Living Index (106).
The Housing category carries the largest weight in a consumer’s budget and typically drives the
overall Comparative Cost of Living Index values. Comparative Cost of Living Index data were
also produced by consumer category for every county. Counties with Housing category index
values above the statewide average in the second quarter of 2018 included Teton (212), Laramie
(107), and Sublette (105).
Counties with lower All Items Comparative Cost of Living Index values in the second quarter of
2018 included Washakie, Niobrara, and Big Horn. These counties have small, rural populations
and typically show lower All Items Comparative Cost of Living Index values.
Comparative Cost of Living Index values for all counties in Wyoming are listed in Table 1 on
page 4. Movement in ranking from a previous survey does not indicate that the price level has
increased or decreased in a particular county. Instead, these values reflect relative price levels in
each county, at the time ofdata collection, compared with the statewide average of 100.
Survey Methodology
The WCLI is produced biannually, the second quarter and fourth quarter. Price data are collected
in twenty-eight cities and towns in Wyoming and the data are used to build a Comparative Cost of
Living Index by county and to estimate overall inflation rates for Wyoming and the five designated

Page 2 of 6

regions of the State. A map of the twenty-eight cities where price data were collected is on page
5.
Communities across the State are included in the WCLI based on the following criteria: First, the
largest city or town in each county is priced; additionally, prices are collected in any city or town
with populations greater than 5,000 or in cases of a city or town having at least 80 percent of a
county’s largest community’s population.
In counties where only one community was priced,
those prices were used to represent the entire county. In counties where two communities were
surveyed, a population-weighted average of the prices for the two communities was used for the
entire county. In addition, starting with the second quarter of 2003 WCLI report, Afton, in Lincoln
County, was added as a sampling point through legislative action in 2003.
Kemmerer is still
priced.
Afton and Kemmerer report separate Comparative Cost of Living Index numbers.
Beginning with the second quarter of 2004, Afton, has been included in the inflation calculations.
The 140 consumer items surveyed were aggregated into six categories, which were then
weighted using item weights from the U.S. Bureau of Labor Statistics (BLS), Consumer Price
Index (CPI-U), reflective of their overall importance in the average consume?s budget. These
categories, and their respective weight components, include Housing (48.1%), Transportation
(16.5%), Food (13.4%), Recreation & Personal Care (9.5%), Medical (8.7%), and Apparel
(3.8%).
The data are weighted by population to more accurately represent the price changes experienced
by the majority of consumers in Wyoming, which showed an overall, statewide inflation rate of
3.3 percent in the second quarter of 2018. Nationally, the inflation rate from June 2017 to June
2018 was 2.9 percent (CPI-U), as reported by the BLS in the Consumer Price Index.
The Division wishes to extend its sincere appreciation to all of the businesses and
individuals whose cooperation and assistance made this survey possible.

Contact: Amy Bittner, Principal Economist
Wyoming Cost of Living Index for the Second Quarter of 2019
CHEYENNE
- The State of Wyoming, Economic Analysis Division has released the second
quarter of 2019 Wyoming Cost of Living Index (WCLI).
Inflation is reported for the State, by
consumer category, and for five regions in the State. Inflation measures year-over-year price
changes in six consumer categories. The inflation figures for the second quarter of2019 represent
the percent change in the price level of a standard basket of selected consumer items priced in the
second quarter of 2019, compared with the price level of the same goods recorded one year ago
(second quarter of 2018).
•
All Items Statewide inflation 2.0%
•
Statewide inflation by consumer category:
Medical 5.0%
Apparel 3.6%
Housing 2.7%
Food 2.3%
Recreation & Personal Care -0.3%
Transportation -0.8%
•
Regional inflation rates:
Northeast 2.3%
Central 2.3%
Southwest 2.2%
Northwest 1.9%
Southeast 1.7%
Nationally, the inflation rate from June 2018 to June 2019 was 1.6 percent (CPI-U), as reported by
the U.S. Bureau of Labor Statistics (BLS) in the Consumer Price Index.
The Wyoming Cost of Living Index (WCLI) consists of two parts, Inflation, which measures
year-over-year change (annual) and the Comparative Cost of Living Index which compares each
county’s cost of living in one period (not over time) to the statewide average. The Comparative
Cost of Living Index represents each county’s price level compared to the statewide average (100)
during a single time period, in this case, the second quarter of 2019.

For the second quarter of 2019, the Comparative Cost of Living Index ranked Teton (161) as the
county with the highest All Items value, which indicates that the cost of living in Teton County
was estimated 61 percent higher than the statewide average in the quarter.
Comparative Cost of Living Index values for all counties in Wyoming are listed in Table 1 on page
3. Movement in ranking from a previous survey does not indicate that the price level has increased
or decreased in a particular county.
Instead, these values reflect relative price levels in each
county, at the time ofdata collection, compared with the statewide average of 100. Comparative
Cost of Living Index data were also produced by consumer category for every county.
Suri’ey Methodology
The WCLI is produced biannually, the second quarter and fourth quarter. Price data are collected
in twenty-eight cities and towns in Wyoming and the data are used to build a Comparative Cost of
Living Index by county and to estimate overall inflation rates for Wyoming and the five designated
regions of the State. A map of the twenty-eight cities where price data were collected is on page
4.
Communities across the State are included in the WCLI based on the following criteria: First, the
largest city or town in each county is priced; additionally, prices are collected in any city or town
with populations greater than 5,000 or in cases of a city or town having at least 80 percent of a
county’s largest community’s population.
In counties where only one community was priced,
those prices were used to represent the entire county. In counties where two communities were
surveyed, a population-weighted average of the prices for the two communities was used for the
entire county. In addition, starting with the second quarter of 2003 WCLI report, Afton, in Lincoln
County, was added as a sampling point through legislative action in 2003.
Kemmerer is still
priced.
Afton and Kemmerer report separate Comparative Cost of Living Index numbers.
Beginning with the second quarter of 2004, Afton, has been included in the inflation calculations.
The 140 consumer items surveyed were aggregated into six categories, which were then weighted
using item weights from the U.S. Bureau of Labor Statistics (BLS), Consumer Price Index (CPI
U), reflective of their overall importance in the average consumer’s budget. These categories, and
their respective weight components, include Housing (48.4%), Transportation (16.3%), Food
(13.3%), Recreation & Personal Care (9.5%), Medical (8.7%), and Apparel (3.8%).
The data are weighted by population to more accurately represent the price changes experienced
by the majority of consumers in Wyoming.
The Division wishes to extend its sincere appreciation to all of the businesses and individuals
whose cooperation and assistance made this survey possible.

ECONOMIC ANALYSIS DIVISION
Mw* Gordon, Governor
Patricia L. Bach, Director
J lien/in Li,,, Administrator
FOR IMMEDIATE RELEASE
October 28, 2020
Contact: Amy Bittner, Principal Economist
Wyoming Cost of Living Index for the Second Quarter of 2020
CHEYENNE
- The second quarter of 2020 Wyoming Cost of Living Index (WCLI) is now available
from the State of Wyoming, Economic Analysis Division.
The WCLI consists of two parts,
Inflation, which measures year-over-year change (annual) and the Comparative Cost of Living
Index which compares each county’s cost of living in one period (not over time) to the statewide
average.
Inflation is reported for the State, by consumer category, and for five regions in the State.
Inflation measures year-over-year price changes in six consumer categories. The inflation figures
for the second quarter of 2020 represent the percent change in the price level of a standard
basket of selected consumer items priced in the second quarter of 2020, compared with the price
level of the same goods recorded one year ago (second quarter of 2019).
•
All Items Statewide inflation 1.1%
•
Statewide inflation by consumer category:
Food 4.9%
Medical 3.0%
Apparel 2.7%
•
Housing 2.6%
•
Recreation & Personal Care. -1.0%
Transportation -7.3%
•
Regional inflation rates:
Southeast 1.8%
•
Central 1.7%
Northwest 0.8%
Southwest 0.2%
Northeast -0.2%
Nationally, the inflation rate from June 2019 to June 2020 was 0.6% (CPI-U), as reported by the
U.S. Bureau of Labor Statistics (BLS) in the Consumer Price Index.
ADMINISTRATION
& IN FORMATION
2800 Central Avenue, Cheyenne, WY 82002
I (307) 777-7504
eadiv.state.wy.us
Page 1 of 5

The Comparative Cost of Living Index represents each county’s price level compared to the
statewide average (100) during a single period, in this case, the second quarter of 2020. For the
second quarter of 2020, the Comparative Cost of Living Index ranked Teton (159) as the county
with the highest All Items value, which indicates that the cost of living in Teton County was
estimated 59 percent higher than the statewide average in the quarter.
Comparative Cost of Living Index values for all counties in Wyoming are listed in Table 1 on page
3.
Movement in ranking from a previous survey does not indicate that the price level has
increased or decreased in a particular county. Instead, these values reflect relative price levels
in each county, at the time of data collection, compared with the statewide average of 100.
Comparative Cost of Living Index data were also produced by consumer category for every
county.
Survey Methodology
The WCLI is produced biannually, the second quarter and the fourth quarter.
Price data are
collected in twenty-eight cities and towns in Wyoming and the data are used to build a
Comparative Cost of Living Index by county and to estimate overall inflation rates for Wyoming
and the five designated regions of the State. A map of the twenty-eight cities where price data
were collected is on page 4.
Communities across the State are included in the WCLI based on the following criteria: First, the
largest city or town in each county is priced; additionally, prices are collected in any city or town
with populations greater than 5,000 or in cases of a city or town having at least 80 percent of a
county’s largest community’s population.
In counties where only one community was priced,
those prices were used to represent the entire county. In counties where two communities were
surveyed, a population-weighted average of the prices for the two communities was used for the
entire county. In addition, starting with the second quarter of 2003 WCLI report, Afton, in Lincoln
County, was added as a sampling point through legislative action in 2003.
Kemmerer is still
priced.
Afton and Kemmerer report separate Comparative Cost of Living Index numbers.
Beginning with the second quarter of 2004, Afton, has been included in the inflation calculations.
The 140 consumer items surveyed were aggregated into six categories, which were then
weighted using item weights from the U.S. Bureau of Labor Statistics (BLS), Consumer Price Index
(CPI-U), reflective of their overall importance in the average consumer’s budget.
These
categories, and their respective weight components, include Housing (48.4%), Transportation
(15.7%), Food (13.8%), Recreation & Personal Care (9.6%), Medical (8.8%), and Apparel (3.6%).
The data are weighted by population to more accurately represent the price changes experienced
by the majority of consumers in Wyoming.
The Division wishes to extend its sincere appreciation to all of the businesses and individuals
whose cooperation and assistance made this survey possible.
